Title: DeepUnfolding_Agent: A Classical_Quantum Adaptive Control API

  • Authors: Shanika Iroshi Nanayakkara, Shiva Raj Pokhrel
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20615
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.20615
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.20615]] DeepUnfolding_Agent: A Classical_Quantum Adaptive Control API(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20615)
  • Keywords: federate, fair
  • Abstract: Adaptive optimization workflows in machine learning, federated learning, quantum machine learning, and hybrid classical quantum systems often embed controller logic directly inside task specific training scripts. This coupling limits reuse, reproducibility, safety validation, and fair comparison across execution environments. This paper presents DeepUnfolding Agent, a schema-driven FastAPI platform that exposes deep unfolded adaptive control as an external software service. External executors register a strategy schema, submit round-level diagnostic variables, receive schema-bounded control actions, execute one optimization round, and return feedback for controller update. The framework separates controller intelligence from domain specific execution through a strategy registry, diagnostic to state adapter, controller factory, safety guard, executor abstraction, feedback update path, SQLite persistence layer, Docker deployment, and reproducible benchmark scripts. The implementation supports classical, quantum-aware, hybrid, Qiskit Aer, and optional IBM quantum processing execution pathways. We validate the software through unit, schema, API, safety, orchestration, Quantum execution, checkpointing, and Docker tests, and demonstrate external user fixed versus adaptive workflows across finance, healthcare, genomics, transport, noisy quantum simulation, and hardware backed IBM Quantum execution. The results show that DeepUnfolding-Agent provides a reusable and traceable adaptive control layer for heterogeneous optimization workflows. Our quantum experiments are interpreted as API level quantum control and hardware portability evidence.

Title: PRISM: PRIvileged Sibling-guided Multi-Agent Topology Design

  • Authors: Phuong Tuan Dat, Qi Li, Xinchao Wang
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20628
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.20628
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.20628]] PRISM: PRIvileged Sibling-guided Multi-Agent Topology Design(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20628)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Multi-agent systems (MAS) based on large language models (LLMs) have become a powerful way to address complex problems, and their effectiveness depends critically on the collaboration topology that decides which agents participate and how they communicate. Recent work learns to generate this topology from the task query, fitting the generator to corpora of execution-verified graphs. The post-training method for such generators remains a limitation: a corpus lists the graphs that solved a query but never ranks them, leaving continued supervised fine-tuning (SFT) with no per-query preference to exploit. Without such a preference, the generator drifts toward the topologies the corpus contains most often rather than those that suit each query, so a gain on one task can come at a cost on another. We propose PRISM, which recovers per-query signal from the same corpus by learning with privileged information: a teacher reads, through a zero-initialized low-rank branch, a sibling graph that also solved the query and is unavailable at deployment, and the student matches its per-decision targets on trajectories the student samples itself. Across six diverse benchmarks PRISM improves over every topology-design baseline evaluated, and matched-teacher controls support the contribution of privileged query information. Code is available in this link.

Title: A Large-Scale Dataset for Multi-Class Financial News Classification in Brazilian Portuguese

  • Authors: Leomax Bandeira, Gabriel Aguiar, Dalton Cézane Gomes Valadares, Luiz Pereira, Helder Fernando de Araújo Oliveira, Gustavo Xavier, Andson M. Balieiro
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20630
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.20630
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.20630]] A Large-Scale Dataset for Multi-Class Financial News Classification in Brazilian Portuguese(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20630)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: The development of Financial Natural Language Processing (NLP) systems for Brazilian Portuguese is hindered by the scarcity of domain-specific datasets. To address this, we present a large-scale corpus of approximately 160,000 Brazilian Portuguese financial news articles collected from web sources. To establish initial benchmarks for a weakly supervised multi-class classification approach based on semantic similarity, we annotated subsets of this corpus both manually and using a Large Language Model (LLM). We evaluated three similarity-based methods -- cosine similarity, K-Nearest Neighbors (KNN), and FAISS-based nearest-neighbor search -- using multilingual semantic embeddings. Our experiments reveal that the task is highly sensitive to label quality and class distribution. On the manually labeled subset, KNN achieved the best performance (47.3% accuracy, 31.9% F1-score), closely followed by FAISS. Conversely, on the LLM-annotated subset, while accuracy reached approximately 50%, F1-scores dropped to near 20%, highlighting the negative impact of label imbalance and noisy weak annotations. These findings establish foundational baselines for a challenging task, underscoring the critical need for high-quality manual annotations. Ultimately, this dataset and our preliminary results provide a reference point for future research in Brazilian Portuguese Financial NLP.

Title: Jev-as-a-Judge for RAG Claim Verification: Confidence-Gated Cascading Between a Non-Generative Verifier and a Frontier LLM

  • Authors: Alden Do Rosario, Kirollos Atef
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20634
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.20634
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.20634]] Jev-as-a-Judge for RAG Claim Verification: Confidence-Gated Cascading Between a Non-Generative Verifier and a Frontier LLM(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20634)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Retrieval-augmented generation asks one question at volume: does this evidence support this claim? The answer is one bit, yet most pipelines obtain it from a text generator and pay generation prices for the prose wrapped around it. We compare a non-generative decision model, which returns a typed value from a declared enumeration together with a native probability and no text, against a frontier LLM configured as an engineer would deploy it for binary classification. Both answer the same 495 human-labelled claims from LLM-AggreFact, so every comparison is within-item. We report three things. First, the two systems are statistically indistinguishable on the metric this task is conventionally scored by, a paired difference of -0.56 points, Jev minus Astra, at a 187-fold cost difference. Second, that tie carries almost no information about deployment: the systems disagree on 15.2% of claims, and the cheaper one admits 23.2% of the claims annotators marked unsupported against the frontier model's 14.1%. A component whose job is to catch unsupported claims should be judged on that rate, and the conventional headline hides it. Third, because the errors differ they compose: routing the claims the cheap model is confident about and escalating the rest reaches 74.10 at USD 2.92 per thousand claims against 73.83 at USD 8.93, with the threshold chosen out of fold. But a matched random-escalation control at in-sample thresholds, spending the same budget by coin flip, does as well, so what buys the saving is the escalation share and not the probability that picks what to escalate. Measured against the cheap model it is built from, the cascade costs 61-fold more for 0.83 points, 95% CI [-3.36, 4.86], and it is the more permissive guard of the two. Equal accuracy conceals unequal errors. All predictions, prompts, pricing and code are released.

Title: Exploring the Design Space of Permutation Diffusion LLMs

  • Authors: Zhe Xu, Mingfu Liang, Kavosh Asadi, Jean-Pascal Billaud, Tianyi Chen, Yanhui Guo, Christina Huang, Baekjin Kim, Jimmy Kim, Yufei Li, Daqi Sun, Jiachen Sun, Dian Yu, Zaiwei Zhang, Kaushik Rangadurai, Frank Shyu, Sandeep Pandey, Xi Liu, Luke Simon
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20635
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.20635
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.20635]] Exploring the Design Space of Permutation Diffusion LLMs(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20635)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Permutation generation with language models requires both text-conditioned ranking and a global one-to-one assignment between candidates and output positions. We study this problem through three diffusion-LLM parameterizations: hard-assignment prediction over shuffled candidate-index sequences, soft-assignment prediction over doubly stochastic matrices, and direct ordering-score prediction followed by sorting. The three families place permutation constraints at different stages of prediction and decoding and therefore support different training objectives and sampling procedures. We evaluate these designs on recommendation reranking across three Amazon domains with candidate sets of size 10 and 20. At one function evaluation, the hard- and soft-assignment variants achieve Recall@1 comparable to a constrained masked-diffusion baseline, whereas direct ordering-score prediction guarantees valid permutations without Hungarian assignment or Sinkhorn normalization. Additional function evaluations do not consistently improve ranking quality, and their effect depends strongly on the state representation and training objective. These results show that corruption, prediction parameterization, objective, and decoder should be designed jointly for permutation diffusion with language-model denoisers.

Title: Expanding Attention: A Taylor-Series Route to Linear-Time Transformers with a Fixed Number of Recurrent States

  • Authors: Zicong Huang
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20637
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.20637
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.20637]] Expanding Attention: A Taylor-Series Route to Linear-Time Transformers with a Fixed Number of Recurrent States(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20637)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: We expand the exponential kernel of attention into a truncated Taylor series of $n$ terms and factorize each term into a tensor-power inner product, which collapses the causal history into exactly $n$ recurrent states, each updated by one rank-one outer product per token and independent of context length $N$. The recursion is lossless with respect to the truncated kernel ($2.2 \times 10^{-16}$ in float64), and the states are additive across chunks ($5.7 \times 10^{-14}$), so prefill admits a parallel scan. Measured exponents are $t \propto N^{2.28}$ for softmax against $N^{0.91}$ for states, with a crossover at $N \approx 728$ and $20.8\times$ at $N = 8192$; per-token decoding latency is constant at $7.3\,\mu$s against $N^{1.04}$ for softmax, giving $76.0\times$ at $N = 65536$. Accuracy improves exponentially in $n$ while cost grows combinatorially, since the $m$-th state is $\binom{d+m-1}{m}$ wide---a bound confirmed by SVD---so the optimum is second order, $n = 3$, at $6.5 \times 10^{-4}$ error and $9{,}945$ elements per head. Query and key normalization is a prerequisite: without it the argument exceeds the convergence radius and adding terms stops helping.

Title: KNOU-Flood: Advancing global streamflow forecasting with lead-aware probabilistic machine learning

  • Authors: Minjong Cheon
  • Subjects: cs.LG, physics.ao-ph
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20640
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.20640
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.20640]] KNOU-Flood: Advancing global streamflow forecasting with lead-aware probabilistic machine learning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20640)
  • Keywords: fair
  • Abstract: Global streamflow forecasting supports flood preparedness, yet data-driven models trained on reanalysis lose skill when driven by operational forecasts, increasingly so with lead time. AIFL addresses this shift with a two-stage transfer-learning recipe and a deterministic LSTM, but it feeds one forecast lead time into the whole input window and gives no uncertainty. We ask how far this open recipe can be extended without changing its recurrent core. KNOU-Flood keeps a single-layer LSTM and the two-stage training and changes the information around it: a longer input window (424 days), lead-aware forecast inputs, GraphCast as a second forecast source next to ECMWF HRES, and a latent-noise ensemble trained with the fair CRPS. We compare against a re-implementation of the AIFL recipe (its code is not public) under identical data, stations, splits and metrics. Our contributions are threefold. First, over three seeds and 2,099 test basins (2021-2023), KNOU-Flood reaches a median KGE' of 0.65 at LT1 and 0.48 at LT10 against 0.62 and 0.34, with significant gains at all ten lead times under two scoring protocols, also against a 1024-unit baseline and on held-out basins (one seed each). Second, identical weights give an LT10 KGE' of 0.18 or 0.34 depending on how forecasts are fed, so much of the lead-time decay reported for AIFL is an input-protocol effect; the probabilistic head contributes most to the gain, followed by the raw GraphCast channels, the longer window and mixed fine-tuning. Third, the ensembles reach lower CRPS than probabilistic versions of the baseline, are nearly calibrated with inference-time dropout, and detect 1.5- and 2-year floods better than all baselines; the benefit for rarer floods depends on the seed, and an exploratory tail-weighted variant improves 10-year and larger events. KNOU-Flood is a controlled extension of an open recipe, not a new architecture.

Title: CoTS: Competitive Token Selection for Latent Reasoning in Large Language Models

  • Authors: Jie Yu, Yuxi Sun, Zhaoxu Li, Chunxiao Liu, Xudong Jiang
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20642
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.20642
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.20642]] CoTS: Competitive Token Selection for Latent Reasoning in Large Language Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20642)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Recent latent reasoning methods move beyond discrete Chain-of-Thought (CoT) by replacing sampled tokens with continuous soft embeddings or hidden states, enabling richer intermediate representations and preserving alternative reasoning paths. However, the need for latent reasoning varies across decoding steps: confident predictions favor discrete commitment, whereas ambiguous steps benefit from preserving multiple plausible alternatives. Existing approaches typically handle this variation through either global soft activation or hard switching based on uncertainty thresholds, treating latent intervention as an all-or-nothing decision rather than continuously adapting its strength. To address this limitation, we propose Competitive Token Selection (CoTS), a training-free framework that reframes latent intervention from binary mode selection into continuous allocation at the token level. CoTS uses the probability competition between the two most likely candidates to dynamically balance discrete commitment and soft exploration at each reasoning step. The second candidate represents the strongest alternative to the model's preferred continuation, so the separation between the two directly reflects the stability of the current discrete choice. When one candidate clearly dominates, CoTS keeps the representation close to the sampled discrete token to preserve a stable reasoning trajectory; as the competition strengthens, it increases the contribution of the soft representation to retain plausible alternatives. This competition-based allocation requires neither manually tuned switching thresholds nor mixing coefficients, and introduces no additional forward passes. Experiments across six reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that CoTS consistently outperforms standard CoT and state-of-the-art training-free latent reasoning methods. Our code is available at this https URL.

Title: Forensic Analysis of the HackRF One with PortaPack Mayhem Firmware

  • Authors: Düzgün Küçük
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20647
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.20647
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.20647]] Forensic Analysis of the HackRF One with PortaPack Mayhem Firmware(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20647)
  • Keywords: attack
  • Abstract: Software-defined radios (SDRs) have become an inexpensive, portable platform for replay attacks, location spoofing and jamming. The HackRF One can operate stand-alone when fitted with a PortaPack interface board running the open-source Mayhem firmware. No peer-reviewed study has addressed the forensic analysis of this device family. This work establishes, at source-code level, where forensically relevant data reside on the microSD card of a device running Mayhem v2.0.1, in which format, when they are written and how they should be interpreted. The methodology is applied to an SD card image that the author acquired from their own device after four months of real use, and every inference is drawn from the image alone. The workflow covers timestamp rules that depend on the real-time clock (RTC) battery state, traces of on-device trimming and text editing, cluster-level recovery of deleted in-phase/quadrature (IQ) captures, and decoding of recorded signals cross-checked with an independent decoder. In the example image, the final part of a deleted IQ capture and a trimmed-away capture segment are recovered, and six captures are attributed to a single rolling-code on-off keying (OOK) transmitter sharing one 32-bit identifier. The study provides practitioners with an artifact catalogue, interpretation rules and acquisition recommendations.

Title: Towards automatic digital garment assembly

  • Authors: Alejandro Rodriguez
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.GR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20651
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.20651
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.20651]] Towards automatic digital garment assembly(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20651)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Creating digital garments from existing two-dimensional patterns remains a time-consuming process. Although patterns are widely available in digital form, their assembly in garment CAD software involves manual definition of sewing operations and arrangement of the pattern panels for drape simulation. We present two learning-based approaches that automate both tasks using the 2D vector pattern as their sole input. Our automatic sewing model operates at the segment level, leveraging a Transformer encoder with multiple prediction heads. Our novel architecture supports many-to-many, ordered segment correspondences, as well as hems, overlapping seams needed for multilayer garments, and construction properties such as seam allowances and stitch types, required for the finished virtual garment but neglected by previous methods. A complementary panel-level model predicts body-region assignments, layers, and placement coordinates to initialize the drape simulation. We evaluate both models on a curated dataset of 4,035 professionally assembled garment instances. The sewing model exactly recovers all seam and hem topologies of 63.3\% of test garments, while the arrangement model meets the specified placement criteria for all panels in 66.2\% of test garments. We also integrate both methods into garment CAD software, demonstrating automatic assembly of successfully predicted garments.

Title: Empirical Evaluation of Machine Learning for Cloud Cryptojacking Detection: Effects of Preprocessing and Evaluation Protocols

  • Authors: Amitabh Chakravorty, Nelly Elsayed
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20658
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.20658
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.20658]] Empirical Evaluation of Machine Learning for Cloud Cryptojacking Detection: Effects of Preprocessing and Evaluation Protocols(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20658)
  • Keywords: attack
  • Abstract: Artificial intelligence (AI) has been widely applied to cloud cryptojacking detection. Classical machine learning models remain among the most widely used approaches for cloud cryptojacking detection. This paper presents an empirical evaluation of six widely used classical machine learning models for AI-based cloud cryptojacking detection under different preprocessing and evaluation settings. Because no public cloud-native cryptojacking dataset is currently available, experiments were conducted using two publicly available proxy datasets, DS2OS and NSL-KDD. The evaluated models include Random Forest, XGBoost, LightGBM, Gradient Boosting, Decision Tree, and K-Nearest Neighbors (KNN). Experimental results show that preprocessing choices and evaluation protocols have a measurable impact on model performance. On DS2OS, removing identifier-related features reduced the accuracy of most models to approximately 96%, while KNN achieved higher accuracy with lower attack recall. On NSL-KDD, models obtained 77 to 80\% accuracy on the official test set containing unseen attack types, compared with above 99.5% under a random train-test split. Attack-class precision, recall, and Matthews Correlation Coefficient (MCC) further provide complementary insights into classifier performance beyond overall accuracy. The results provide an empirical comparison of commonly used classical machine learning models under consistent experimental settings and offer insights into how preprocessing, dataset selection, and evaluation methodology influence reported performance.

Title: Hybrid DeepXAI Fusion Network for Interpretable Big Data Analytics and Secure Cloud Decision-Making Systems

  • Authors: B. Nancharaiah, Ramakrishna Kolikipogu, J. Sulthan Alikhan, Lakshmana Phaneendra Maguluri, Elangovan Muniyandy, D. Baburao

  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20661

Title: TurboMamba: Faster Inference, Faster Training, and Better Prediction with Lexical Memory

  • Authors: Hanxin Zhang
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20682
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.20682
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.20682]] TurboMamba: Faster Inference, Faster Training, and Better Prediction with Lexical Memory(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20682)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: We introduce TurboMamba, a language-model architecture for faster inference, faster training, and better token prediction. A global Mamba-3 model runs once per completed token block. A small Transformer uses this global context and earlier tokens within the block to predict the next token. Three lightweight additions improve local predictions: direct access to the previous block's last token, a small correction to next-token scores, and a learned mixture with recent-token frequencies. We compare twelve small-scale implementations on public FineWeb-Edu, with comparable parameter counts and shared training-budget caps. The baselines include Transformer, Mamba-3, Jamba, and Zamba. The Jamba and Zamba versions use Mamba-3 in place of their original Mamba modules. TurboMamba achieves the lowest mean test prediction loss (negative log-likelihood, NLL) among all twelve models. Its mean NLL is 9.4% lower than that of Mamba-3, 7.3% lower than that of the Jamba version, and 18.3% lower than that of the Zamba version. End-to-end training throughput is 2.60x that of Mamba-3, 2.81x that of the Jamba version, and 4.82x that of the Zamba version. We measure end-to-end batch latency for a batch of 32 sequences, each with a 256-token prompt and 128 generated tokens. On this workload, TurboMamba achieves a 3.12x speedup over Mamba-3, a 3.53x speedup over the Jamba version, and a 6.21x speedup over the Zamba version.

Title: Causal Syntax Circuits in CodeLMs: Budget and Probe Decide What Looks Localised

  • Authors: Harsh Kumar, Tanmay Shailesh Joshi, Rahul Maity, Anusa Saha
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.SE
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20683
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.20683
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.20683]] Causal Syntax Circuits in CodeLMs: Budget and Probe Decide What Looks Localised(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20683)
  • Keywords: extraction
  • Abstract: Code models write Python when the prompt does not say which language to use. We ask a mechanistic question about that habit: is it produced by a small, findable group of components inside the model? We run one pipeline---mean ablation, activation patching, greedy circuit extraction, a random-control significance test, and activation steering---over 55 model-language settings spanning ten open code models (five base and five instruction-tuned), three target languages (Rust, C#, PHP), two prompt suites, and two component budgets. We report five findings. First, the preference is large, grows with model size, and survives instruction tuning: it is larger on every instruction-tuned checkpoint than on its base version, by 12% on average. Second, its measured size is mostly a property of the probe; replacing our aligned prompts with XLCoST parallel functions takes the same measurement on the same models from about 1.6 to about 0.01. Third, whether the preference looks localised depends on how many components the search may keep: with a budget of 12, 1 of 15 settings yields a circuit that beats size-matched random controls after multiple-comparison correction, and with a budget of 40, 6 of 15 do. Fourth, interventions that a smaller-budget study reports as uniformly failing do sometimes succeed: patching makes a Python prompt prefer target syntax in 2 of 45 settings, and steering changes the most likely next token in 9 of 45. Fifth, every circuit whose effect is significant but points away from the target language comes from a setting where circuit extraction failed, which makes such results a diagnostic rather than a phenomenon. The practical lesson is that a claim about whether a behaviour is localised can turn on analysis choices that papers rarely report. We release the full grid so those choices can be varied.

Title: RoLaQ: Joint Rotation and Range Calibration for 2-Bit KV Caches in Multi-Head Latent Attention

  • Authors: Shiyu Ji, Peng Ling, Chen Lin, Shupeng Li, Yixuan Wang, Qingfu Zhu, Wanxiang Che
  • Subjects: cs.LG, stat.ML
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20686
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.20686
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.20686]] RoLaQ: Joint Rotation and Range Calibration for 2-Bit KV Caches in Multi-Head Latent Attention(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20686)
  • Keywords: protect
  • Abstract: Multi-head latent attention (MLA) compresses the content of a key-value cache into a shared latent per token, making low-bit storage attractive for long-context inference. At two bits, errors in this representation perturb both attention matching and value readout, with effects that depend on error direction and reconstruction scale. We introduce RoLaQ (Rotation-calibrated Latent Quantization), an offline method for joint rotation and range calibration. RoLaQ refines a shared rotation within subspaces defined by the frozen key and value projections, and jointly learns quantization ranges whose thresholds and reconstruction gains are coupled to control the shared signal's response. Prediction matching and attention-output supervision guide calibration with model weights frozen. Across three MLA models and six benchmarks, RoLaQ achieves the highest average accuracy among the compared 2-bit methods both without and with token protection. On DeepSeek-V2-Lite, an SGLang implementation provides 3.96 times the BF16 KV capacity under the same memory budget and 2.51 times its decode throughput with 256K input tokens.

Title: On-Policy Residual Learning for Diffusion Language Models

  • Authors: Yifan Yu, Yuqing Jian, Jisen Li, Yuezhou Hu, Tianyi Zhang, Xiaoxia Wu, Rui-Jie Zhu, Junxiong Wang, Zhongzhu Zhou, Fan Lai, James Zou, Chenfeng Xu
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20687
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.20687
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.20687]] On-Policy Residual Learning for Diffusion Language Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20687)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Residual context is key to unlocking the speed-quality Pareto frontier of diffusion language models (DLMs) by carrying forward predictive distributions across denoising steps. Yet turning this seemingly simple mechanism into an effective learning paradigm is far from simple: there is no clear recipe for how residual context should be learned. We introduce ORL, a framework for on-policy residual learning in DLMs. Algorithmically, we identify that residual learning is inherently on-policy. ORL learns from model-induced residual trajectories on the fly and introduces an entropy-contraction objective that sharpens predictions as the model revisits its own predictions. System-wise, exact residual supervision requires capturing, retaining, and reprocessing many trajectory-dependent states. ORL co-designs the algorithm with asynchronous residual capture and transport, specialized block-diffusion attention, and packed compute-balanced training, making it practical at scale. On a 4B DLM converted from Qwen3-4B, ORL improves tokens per forward (TPF) by up to 6.2x and, with our dlmrt engine, end-to-end throughput by approximately 6.9x at comparable or better quality. In single-request serving on one H100, this mode achieves a stronger speed-quality Pareto frontier than autoregressive models with EAGLE3 and DFlash speculative decoding under both strict and relaxed verification, running 1.74x faster than DFlash at matched accuracy. ORL also transfers to existing DLMs: on LLaDA-2.1-mini, 36 H100-hours and 4.2M self-generated tokens, about 10x less data than prior LLaDA recipes, yield up to 1.5x higher TPF and up to 3x higher throughput at comparable quality, and on DiffusionGemma, ORL raises TPF by 1.1-1.2x. With dlmrt and NVFP4 quantization, ORL models decode a single request at up to 5,046 tokens/s on one B200, 7,708 on eight B200s, and 2,788 on a consumer RTX 5090, with comparable quality.

Title: WorldCast: Exploring Intelligent Visual Generation in World State Prediction

  • Authors: Wenhao Yan, Haodong Li, Xiaotong Li, Yaxin Luo, Zhengrong Yue, Jialv Zou, Xu Huang, Haojin Yang, Nan Jia, Yan Feng, Peng Pei, Xunliang Cai

  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20731
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.20731

  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Humans interpret multimodal observations of the world and forecast how it will evolve. Recent multimodal generative systems have demonstrated similar capabilities: they can integrate multimodal context and synthesize visual outputs that depict changes in the world. To characterize this emerging trajectory, we organize model capabilities into a four-level hierarchy and discover that higher-level tasks remain relatively underexplored despite model progress. To bridge this gap, we explore model performance on multi-step world state prediction, a high-level task that requires models to forecast successive future world states from multimodal observations and diverse references. Such a task requires models to possess entangled understanding and generation capabilities, as well as extensive world knowledge to translate human action sequences, physical transformations, and abstract visual reasoning into coherent visual outputs. We introduce WorldCast, a benchmark comprising 320 manually designed cases across eight categories, and evaluate 12 representative models. These findings highlight the need for multimodal generative models that are capable of dynamic planning and reasoning while also being deeply unified to faithfully simulate real-world states.

Title: Quantile-Guided Diffusion for Probabilistic Time-Series Forecasting

  • Authors: Shadi Omari, Bashar Khoury, Assaf Schuster
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20739
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.20739
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.20739]] Quantile-Guided Diffusion for Probabilistic Time-Series Forecasting(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20739)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Quantile forecasts summarize uncertainty at each future time step, while diffusion forecasters represent it through sampled future trajectories. We study how predicted quantiles can serve as distributional controls for diffusion forecasting. Specifically, we investigate how the mechanism of quantile integration affects its effectiveness in guiding a pretrained diffusion forecaster. We find that residual control throughout the denoising network yields the most consistent improvements. We instantiate this approach as Control-TS, which learns a quantile-control pathway while keeping the pretrained backbone frozen. Across eight benchmarks, Control-TS consistently improves the backbone's CRPS, achieves competitive performance against state-of-the-art forecasting models, and retains its gains in capacity-matched comparisons. Further analyses examine how these gains depend on quantile granularity, predictor choice, and forecast quality. Finally, we introduce the Conditional Linear Predictive Score (CLPS), which complements distributional metrics by evaluating the downstream forecasting utility of generated futures. Together, these findings support predicted quantiles as an effective interface between explicit uncertainty estimates and diffusion-based trajectory generation.

Title: Interpreting Language Model Parameters

  • Authors: Lucius Bushnaq, Dan Braun, Oliver Clive-Griffin, Bart Bussmann, Nathan Hu, Michael Ivanitskiy, Linda Linsefors, Lee Sharkey
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20748
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.20748
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.20748]] Interpreting Language Model Parameters(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20748)
  • Keywords: interpretability
  • Abstract: Mechanistic interpretability aims to uncover how neural networks use their parameters to implement their impressive neural algorithms. Although previous work has uncovered substantial structure in the intermediate representations that networks use, little progress has been made to understand how the parameters and nonlinearities of networks perform computations on those representations. In this work, we present a method that brings us closer to this understanding by decomposing a language model's parameters into subcomponents that each implement only a small part of the model's learned algorithm, while simultaneously requiring only a small fraction of those subcomponents to account for the network's behavior on any input. The method, adVersarial Parameter Decomposition (VPD), optimizes for decompositions of neural network parameters into simple subcomponents that preserve the network's input-output behavior even when many subcomponents are ablated, including under ablations that are adversarially selected to destroy behavior. This encourages learning subcomponents that provide short, mechanistically faithful descriptions of the network's behavior that should aggregate appropriately into more global descriptions of the network's learned algorithm. We study how sequences of interactions between these parameter subcomponents produce the network's output on particular inputs, enabling a new kind of 'circuit' analysis. While more work remains to be done to deepen our understanding of how neural networks use their parameters to compute their behavior, our work suggests an approach to identify a small set of simple, mechanistically faithful subcomponents on which further mechanistic analysis can be based.

Title: Optimal Hydra: Model Heterogeneous Federated Learning for LLMs using Data-Informed Hydra-Nets

  • Authors: Gwen Legate, Rahaf Aljundi, Eugene Belilovsky
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20753
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.20753
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.20753]] Optimal Hydra: Model Heterogeneous Federated Learning for LLMs using Data-Informed Hydra-Nets(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20753)
  • Keywords: federate, transformer, large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) trained on vast amounts of data have demonstrated impressive performance over a range of tasks, but future performance gains will require increasing amounts of data and compute. Federated Learning (FL) facilitates training on decentralized private data, but client resource limitations can make training impractical, particularly as model sizes continue to expand. These resource constraints make the assumption of a shared server-client architecture impractical. Model heterogeneous FL offers a promising alternative by adapting model size to client capabilities; however, previous work is limited to the pre-training setting, and conclusions on sub-network selection do not necessarily hold for fine-tuning. Furthermore, prior approaches create client sub-networks without considering their unique data distributions, thereby overlooking the opportunity to tailor sub-network architecture to local data. Motivated by evidence that attention heads exhibit task dependency within layer defined feature spaces, we propose Optimal Hydra, a transformer based heterogeneous FL framework for fine-tuning that combines global layer selection with client specific attention head selection, aligned with local data distributions. Optimal Hydra makes explicit use of client data to produce client-specific sub-networks we call Hydra-nets. To our knowledge, this is the first model heterogeneous FL approach tailored to LLM fine-tuning. Experiments on three datasets show significant improvements over baseline methods while also reducing local resource demands.

Title: Foundations of Hardware-Software Leakage Contracts

  • Authors: Jan Reineke
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20754
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.20754
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.20754]] Foundations of Hardware-Software Leakage Contracts(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20754)
  • Keywords: secure, security, attack
  • Abstract: Microarchitectural attacks such as Spectre and Meltdown undermine security by exploiting software-visible side effects of microarchitectural optimizations like caches and speculative execution. To use modern hardware securely, programmers must take into account the security implications of these optimizations. Yet instruction set architectures (ISAs)---the conventional abstraction layer between hardware and software---are ill-suited for secure programming: by design, they abstract from microarchitectural details and therefore fail to reflect their security consequences. A new security abstraction is emerging to fill this void: hardware-software leakage contracts. To support the principled development of secure software on modern hardware such contracts characterize side-channel leakage at the ISA level. Thereby they enable programmers and compilers to generate code that is provably resistant to side-channel attacks---without having to explicitly reason about the microarchitecture. On the other hand, they provide a specification for hardware designers to ensure that microarchitectures do not introduce unintended and undocumented side channels. This paper introduces the foundations of this emerging topic, discussing recent contributions and unresolved challenges along the way.

Title: Purify Before You Align: Improving Representation Alignment for Diffusion Models

  • Authors: Yingheng Wang, Yaoqiang Li, Yaqin Wu, Junwen Bai, Jiatao Gu, Christopher De Sa, Volodymyr Kuleshov
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20761
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.20761
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.20761]] Purify Before You Align: Improving Representation Alignment for Diffusion Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20761)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Representation alignment improves and accelerates diffusion training by encouraging intermediate features to match a pretrained visual encoder. However, standard alignment matches pretrained representations without distinguishing what information may be more useful for generation. We introduce local appearance purification (LAP), which improves generation quality and makes encoders such as MAE more effective alignment targets by removing locally predictable content and aligning to the residual. LAP modifies only the alignment target, and requires no changes to diffusion training or inference. Our theory gives a variational interpretation of standard alignment and, for fixed local predictors, a sum of token-wise information bounds conditioned on local appearance. To understand why purification benefits some encoders more than others, we derive an exact constant-readout test that measures how well the target can be matched without using the diffusion model's hidden state. LAP improves generation quality across five pretrained representations (MAE, AIMv2, CLIP, DINOv2, DINOv3), with the largest gain for MAE: on ImageNet-256 with SiT-B/2, it reduces FID from $53.1$ to $42.3$. Title: GhostLock: Learning-Assisted Blind GPS Signal Nullification Without Victim-Side Information

  • Authors: Khalil Ahmad, Omar Ansari, Mohsin Kamal, Asif Iqbal, Muhammad Naveed Aman

  • Abstract: The Global Positioning System (GPS) provides critical positioning, navigation, and timing (PNT) services for numerous safety- and mission-critical applications, making it an attractive target for physical-layer attacks. While GPS spoofing and jamming have been extensively studied, GPS Signal Nullification (GSN)-a physical-layer attack that suppresses authentic satellite signals through destructive interference prior to acquisition-remains largely unexplored. Existing wireless signal nullification techniques target conventional communication systems and typically require victim-side information, such as channel estimates or synchronization parameters, making them impractical for GPS. Furthermore, many GPS spoofing defenses assume authentic satellite signals remain available as trusted references, an assumption invalidated by GSN. To address this gap, we present GhostLock, a learning-assisted blind GSN framework requiring only an approximate target location estimate. We implement GhostLock on a real-time software-defined radio (SDR) platform using GPS-SDR-SIM and GNU Radio. Experimental results demonstrate successful suppression of GPS signal acquisition, establishing the feasibility of blind GSN and motivating new detection and mitigation techniques for this emerging GPS threat model.

Title: Denoising Score Matching in Diffusion Models: The Effect of Rank Constraints on Generalization

  • Authors: Anand Jerry George, Nicolas Macris
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20850
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.20850
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.20850]] Denoising Score Matching in Diffusion Models: The Effect of Rank Constraints on Generalization(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20850)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Architectural biases restrict the class of functions that a diffusion score network can represent, but their effect on generalization remains poorly understood. We study denoising score matching with score networks having a rank constrained non-linear term and a linear skip connection. Numerical experiments with trainable three-layer networks on data supported on an embedded sphere show that increasing rank lowers training error, but its effect on test error depends on sample size and data dimension. To explain this behavior, we analyze a random-feature score model under rank constraint with Gaussian data supported on a low-dimensional subspace. We characterize the optimal rank-constrained estimator through a truncated singular value decomposition and derive asymptotically precise limiting training and test errors when sample size $(n)$, feature width $(p)$, data dimension $(D)$, and rank $(r)$ scale proportionally with the ambient dimension $(d)$. The resulting spectral characterization distinguishes eigenmodes that improve the empirical fit from those that improve population accuracy. It also identifies the dimension of the orthogonal complement of the data subspace as a natural rank threshold. The theoretical learning curves qualitatively reproduce the observed rank-dependent overfitting, providing a tractable account of how bottlenecks or rank constraints can regularize score estimation and why their effectiveness depends on data dimension and sample size.

  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.LG

  • Keywords: segmentation
  • Abstract: Typology-aware tokenizers are motivated by the claim that segmentation which respects morpheme boundaries preserves information that frequency-driven segmentation destroys. Studies routinely establish that it helps while assuming the boundaries must be linguistically correct. We separate them. Tagalog marks voice with infixes (s-um-ulat 'wrote', s-in-ulat 'was written'), so the root is not contiguous and no sequence of BPE merges can recover it. We build MorphAustro-Tok, a tokenizer that emits the root as a contiguous unit followed by a positional infix token, and compare it against a corpus-matched BPE baseline and, at two of three fertility levels, against a scrambled-morpheme control that preserves morph count, invertibility and per-type consistency while randomising the pseudo-infix. Token count does not explain the gain, since it grows as segmentation coarsens, and neither does capacity: at the coarsest level the scrambled arm carries more parameters and scores lower. Scrambling the analysis moves the gain by less than between-seed dispersion at both controlled levels, so we detect no contribution from morphological correctness at this scale.

Scoping each decision to a graph result rather than re-reading the engagement cut the reasoning payload by about 98% on a token proxy. We give a reproducible setup for Claude Code and Codex.   • Authors: Guanghui Min, Chen Chen
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20927
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.20927

  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Long-horizon LLM agents rely on context compaction: when the interaction history outgrows the context window, a compressor replaces it with a natural-language summary. We show that these summaries contain more than a record of what happened. Compaction prompts ask for decisions and next steps, so each summary mixes facts established by the environment with reasoning written by the compressor, and the agent cannot tell the two apart afterwards. In an analysis of a widely used structured compressor, 83% of failed runs trace back to an action that carries out a specific summary entry, most of which no environment output supports. We propose Source-Judged Compaction (SJC), a training-free post-processing step that scores each summary entry for whether it has a source in the environment outputs or the previous summary, and removes the entries that do not. It changes neither the agent nor the compressor and adds one inexpensive judge request per compaction. The judge is Jev, a non-generative decision model that answers the source question with a calibrated probability instead of text. On AppWorld and OfficeBench with two agent models, SJC improves cross-run reliability over the underlying compressor by 2.1%-8.9% without increasing the agent's token usage.

  • Abstract: We present Screwtape, a pipeline that converts real agent transcripts into attacks that evade action-only monitors. The edited actions are never executed, so we use an LLM verifier to reject attacks that are inconsistent with the original transcript or below a severity bar. The attacker revises the edit until the monitor no longer flags it. We applied Screwtape to 94 Claude Code and Codex transcripts from Redwood Research staff, optimizing against our own monitors. We expect to find more attacks when the pipeline runs inside the developer's infrastructure, with frontier attacker models that lack refusal safeguards and direct optimization against the production monitor. Developers can use these attacks to fix monitor blind spots, and evaluators can use them as counterexamples to safety claims about the monitor.

Title: Does Client Diversity Protect the Lightning Network? Evidence from the 2026 Core Lightning Shutdown

  • Authors: Saurabh Kumar
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20952
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.20952

  • Keywords: security, protect
  • Abstract: Client diversity is often cited as the Lightning Network's defence against a bug in any one implementation, yet LND runs 84% of public nodes and holds 84% of public channel capacity, a share that has barely moved since 2023. Using 805 daily gossip snapshots, 132,360 on-chain channel closes and a client classifier validated against self-identifying aliases and against the wallet that builds each node's funding transactions, we find that 22% of CLN nodes went dark overnight, ten times the usual rate, mostly Tor-reachable ones; a third were still dark five weeks later. Losing ACINQ's Eclair node alone costs the surviving network four times the reachability lost with all 619 routable CLN nodes, and minority clients' reliance on LND for connectivity is what their size implies; CLN nodes in fact peer with each other more than chance.   • Keywords: security, attack, generative, large language model
  • Abstract: NVIDIA's Grace Hopper (GH200) superchip represents a new generation of heterogeneous computing, tightly integrating a 72-core Arm-based CPU with a Hopper GPU on a single module via a high-bandwidth, cache-coherent interconnect. By eliminating the conventional PCIe bottleneck for CPU-GPU data movement, this design establishes a unified memory domain that scales to the capacities demanded by generative AI and high-performance computing (HPC) workloads. The architecture also introduces hardware-assisted page migration: driven by access counters, the system dynamically promotes frequently accessed pages from host CPU memory to GPU DRAM to improve memory locality. We study the security of this emerging architecture against new side- and covert-channel attacks targeting its page migration support. Using proof-of-concept attacks, we build covert channels that reach roughly 8,500 bits per second through eviction-based signaling and 8,100 bits per second through migration-delay-based signaling across MIG instances. The interrupt-based channel also fingerprints inference workloads across distinct large language models (LLMs) and CPU SPEC2017 benchmarks, and estimates the prompt length processed during LLM inference with high accuracy. These results expose a security gap in hardware-managed heterogeneous memory systems and call into question the isolation guarantees of current MIG-enabled multi-tenant GPU deployments.

After receiving the complete and unified 3D scene information, the agent chooses reasoning or computational tools to solve the spatial problem.   • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Data selection reallocates training support across visual concepts, including objects, attributes, color, texture, and backgrounds. This work explicitly investigates this relationship across fine-grained, spurious-correlation, and general image classification, as well as multimodal pretraining. Specifically, we use sparse autoencoders (SAEs) to extract concept activations from individual images. During selection, we accumulate the dataset-level concept support for each added sample. We then incorporate local support to find samples that reliably strengthen under-supported concepts. Empirical results show consistent improvements over strong data selection baselines across all benchmarks. We also reveal the connection between concepts our method enhances and the induced behavioral changes, including improved robustness against out-of-distribution and spurious contexts. This work establishes concept support, beyond individual sample importance, as a useful principle for data-efficient and robust learning.

  • Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents for health can accumulate reusable textual skills from task feedback, such as calculation criteria, measurement time points, and formula units. Deciding which skills to retain is consequential: useful rules improve later answers, while faulty generalizations propagate errors. We propose CausalEvo, a method for causal skill self-evolution that estimates the relative effects of deliberately changed skill sets while holding the model weights, feedback cases, and rule count fixed. At each evolution step, a frozen LLM proposes candidate rules, which are pooled with the incumbent skills so that the current skill set remains a feasible choice. Paired skill replacements against shared background rules are scored on the same feedback cases; least squares converts the score differences into relative skill values, and the top-ranked rules are retained, favoring incumbents on ties. A decision-loss bound separates candidate support, approximation, and estimation errors. On six MedCalc-Bench-Verified tasks with 120 held-out cases, CausalEvo achieves 60.00% accuracy, compared with 55.83% for SkillOpt and 51.67% without skills. Across the recorded training configurations, which use different optimization budgets, it consumes 55.6% fewer training tokens than SkillOpt. Gains concentrate in particular calculations alongside regressions on others, and paired accuracy intervals include zero. CausalEvo turns task feedback into controlled decisions about skill retention and replacement. Project repository: this https URL

Title: Blind Watermarking of Software Repositories via Module Placement

  • Authors: Khadija Mohamed, Khaled Fathy, Mamdouh Farouk, Mohamed M. Darwish
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21031
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.21031
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.21031]] Blind Watermarking of Software Repositories via Module Placement(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21031)
  • Keywords: extraction, watermark
  • Abstract: Source-code watermarking supports provenance tracking by embedding a recoverable identifier in a software artifact. Many existing approaches encode this identifier in tokens or program syntax, exposing the watermark to transformations that modify the source text. We present PlaceMark, a blind watermarking system that encodes a payload in the placement of modules within a repository. Each eligible module is identified by its filename and top-level definition names. A secret key determines its ordered candidate destinations and its position in the reading order; the module's location within its candidate list determines the encoded symbol. Changes to filenames or definition names alter the identity input and are measured separately. Exact recovery also depends on placement feasibility. The results characterize module placement as a carrier that can complement watermarks embedded in source text.

Title: INCONVENIENT: A Benchmark to Assess LLM Generalization Capabilities from Convenience Samples

  • Authors: Sara Rezaeimanesh, Chad Klochko, Kundan Thind, Mohammad Ghassemi
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21038
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.21038
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.21038]] INCONVENIENT: A Benchmark to Assess LLM Generalization Capabilities from Convenience Samples(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21038)
  • Keywords: robust, large language model
  • Abstract: Machine learning models for tabular prediction typically assume that training data are representative of the population on which they will be deployed. In practice, however, data are often collected based on convenience, availability, or cost, resulting in systematic sampling biases that can distort feature--target relationships and undermine real-world generalization. Existing tabular distribution-shift benchmarks primarily construct out-of-distribution (OOD) settings by partitioning observations across naturally occurring domains. While such partitions may induce changes in P(Y | X), the resulting shifts are neither explicitly controlled nor isolated, and the evaluation distributions themselves are often not representative of the broader target population. We introduce INCONVENIENT, a benchmark designed to study robustness to convenience-sample bias. Rather than relying on naturally occurring domain partitions, INCONVENIENT directly perturbs feature--target relationships in the training distribution while preserving the original population distribution as the deployment environment. This setting more closely reflects real-world scenarios in which models are developed from unrepresentative samples and subsequently deployed on a broader population. The distinction is particularly relevant for large language models (LLMs), whose pretraining may encode population-level knowledge that extends beyond the relationships observed in a biased training dataset. Unlike existing distribution-shift benchmarks, INCONVENIENT provides explicit control over both the direction and severity of relationship shifts, enabling a systematic assessment of robustness to convenience sampling. The benchmark comprises 200 tabular classification and regression datasets spanning diverse application domains and generates around 15,500 shifted training distributions which lead to substantial downstream degradation.

Title: SALTO: Solver-Anchored Lifted Transfer Operators for Nonlinear Event Inference

  • Authors: Qi Wang, Rylie Foster
  • Subjects: cs.LG, math.DS
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21049
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.21049
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.21049]] SALTO: Solver-Anchored Lifted Transfer Operators for Nonlinear Event Inference(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21049)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Learned dynamical models can accurately predict trajectories while producing unreliable sensitivities when differentiated or transposed, limiting their use in inverse problems and scientific decision-making. Classical adjoints provide exact local derivatives, but a single adjoint cannot represent the nonlinear response of a system to a family of finite-amplitude perturbations. We introduce SALTO (Solver-Anchored Lifted Transfer Operators), a Koopman-based framework for inferring finite-amplitude sources. In the lifted space, the nonlinear dynamics are approximated by a linear propagator, and its transpose carries each sensor backward in a single solve. The result is a reusable lifted adjoint that predicts the measurement produced by any candidate localized event with one encoder pass and an inner product. To give the model the simulator's exact local behavior, SALTO imposes three structural constraints: it anchors the physical-state dynamics to the simulator's linearized propagator, centers the learned observables so that they vanish to second order at the reference state, and uses a block-triangular lifted propagator. These constraints guarantee that the model reduces to the simulator's tangent dynamics and classical adjoint as the amplitude vanishes, while the learned observables carry only the finite-amplitude correction. On three nonlinear PDE systems, SALTO predicts the measurements of finite-amplitude sources to within 2-8% where the classical adjoint is in error by 41-165%, recovers source position and intensity from sparse sensors nearly as accurately as inversion through the solver, and samples the joint posterior of two interacting sources, matching the solver's posterior on reaction-diffusion at a twentieth of its cost. One trained model serves new sensor layouts and measurement times with a single backward solve.

  • Abstract: Score-based diffusion methods usually perturb data with Brownian Gaussian noise, corresponding to a light-tailed forward stochastic differential equation (SDE). We study STOCHLOB, a training-only score-matching framework that changes the forward diffusion corruption law while leaving the deployed predictor deterministic.   • Abstract: Post-hoc image explanations usually show where a model looks using spatial heatmaps or indicate which channels matter using global channel scores. Title: Noise-Space Reward Alignment for Few-Step Generative Models

  • Authors: Woocheol Shin, Taeyoung Yun, Jiwon Sung, Hyeongyu Kang, Jeongmin Park, Jaewoo Lee, Kyuil Sim, Dohyun Kim, Yutong He, Jinkyoo Park

  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21125
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.21125
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.21125]] Noise-Space Reward Alignment for Few-Step Generative Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21125)
  • Keywords: diffusion, generative
  • Abstract: Few-step generative models produce high-quality samples in one or a few evaluations. However, steering them toward desired properties remains difficult, especially when only black-box reward evaluations are available. While one could apply approaches developed for iterative diffusion and flow-based models, such as direct RL fine-tuning or test-time scaling, we find that neither transfers well to the few-step regime: direct fine-tuning suffers from severe reward over-optimization, and test-time scaling sacrifices the efficiency advantage of few-step generation. We propose Noise Policy Optimization (NPO), which explicitly targets the alignment problem for few-step generative models. The key idea is to learn an amortized flow policy over the initial noise space of the generator. The trained policy transports Gaussian noise to a reward-tilted noise distribution, whose pushforward distribution through the frozen generator is exactly the reward-tilted output distribution. By keeping the generator frozen, we can effectively utilize the knowledge encoded in the pretrained model, yielding a better reward-quality trade-off than direct fine-tuning. The amortized policy moves the search cost into training, so inference adds only a lightweight noise sampling step on top of the pretrained model. To improve sample efficiency during training, we use ELBO-based likelihood estimation that requires only sampled endpoints rather than simulated trajectories. Across image, language, and protein structure generation, NPO consistently achieves a better reward-quality trade-off than direct fine-tuning, and matches test-time search methods with 16 to 549 times lower inference cost.

Title: Reaction Attack on CKKS Deployments with Asymptotically Optimal Query Complexity

  • Authors: Song Bian, Hyeongmin Choe, Jiseung Kim, Changmin Lee
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21127
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.21127
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.21127]] Reaction Attack on CKKS Deployments with Asymptotically Optimal Query Complexity(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21127)
  • Keywords: security, attack
  • Abstract: We study a protocol-level reaction leakage in CKKS deployments where public coefficient-to-slot (C2S) evaluation keys are combined with a client's 1-bit validity response. Under this deployment surface, a malicious evaluator can recover a ternary secret key using asymptotically optimal $O(H)$ reaction queries, where $H$ is the entropy of the secret key. The attack does not break RLWE or CKKS IND-CPA security. Instead, it exploits the interaction between client-side validity reactions and public homomorphic linear transformations. A shift of a public encryption key yields a ciphertext whose plaintext is a function of the secret key; the public ${\sf C2S}$ layer then repacks this information to construct a ciphertext whose validity depends strictly on whether a targeted secret coefficient matches a guessed value. Furthermore, each submitted query is independently randomized with a fresh encryption of zero, so that each resulting malicious ciphertext appears entirely distinct, thereby allowing the attack to simply evade trivial detection mechanisms on the client side. We implemented the full end-to-end attack in Lattigo v6.2.0 and recovered the full secret key across four parameter sets drawn from standard CKKS presets. For uniform ternary secrets, the attack uses $O(N)$ queries and is within a factor $2/\log_2 3 < 2$ of the oracle-only entropy lower bound. For sparse ternary secrets, a tailored variant further reduces the number of queries in practice. Our results show that observable validity reactions form a practical key-recovery channel in CKKS deployments exposing this surface, even when the underlying cryptographic assumptions remain intact.

  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21128

  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Label-free evaluation methods, such as self-consistency filtering, majority-vote aggregation, and agreement-based reliability scoring, rely on an implicit assumption that consistency can serve as a proxy for correctness when ground-truth labels are unavailable or expensive to obtain. In this paper, we test this assumption directly. Using four financial QA datasets (multiple-choice and free-form), ten consistency dimensions, and two frontier model families, we measure the joint distribution of consistency and accuracy at the level of individual examples rather than in aggregate. We find that 60% of all incorrect answers fall in the high-consistency region (consistency $\geq$ 0.8), and that 77.7% of these show complete answer collapse, i.e. identical wrong answers across all perturbation variants. We show that the phenomenon is robust to the choice of consistency threshold t (it persists from t=0.50 to t=1.00), is shared across model families, and is concentrated in specific intersections of perturbation dimension and domain. We map where this quadrant occurs, analyze its stability properties, and discuss the implications for evaluation pipelines that use agreement-based signals in place of human labels.

Title: Sparse Attention for Video Generation Acceleration via Growing Sparsity and Reduced Search

  • Authors: Yun Dai, Yuyun Chen, Jiarui Wen, Huiping Zhuang, Cen Chen, Ziqian Zeng

  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21129

  • Keywords: diffusion, transformer
  • Abstract: Diffusion Transformers (DiTs) dominate video generation but suffer from high inference cost due to the quadratic complexity of self-attention. To address these limitations, we analyze attention dynamics through attention entropy and mean attention distance, and find that attention becomes increasingly concentrated across denoising steps. GSRS adopts Top-p selection for adaptive sparsity control, supported by a precise evaluation scheme that computes exact attention weights within the FlashAttention computation at negligible overhead.   • Abstract: Proactive dialogue agents must guide multi-turn interactions toward task goals while adapting to users whose intentions and attitudes evolve. Title: From Long Context to Selective Memory:A Survey of Memory-Centric Compression for Large Language Models

  • Authors: Chaogui Gou
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21165
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.21165
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.21165]] From Long Context to Selective Memory:A Survey of Memory-Centric Compression for Large Language Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21165)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Long-context capability has become essential for large language models (LLMs) to handle complex tasks, yet continuously expanding the context window does not remove the computational, storage, and information-selection pressures incurred at inference time. Existing work on prompt compression, KV-cache management, efficient serving, retrieval augmentation, and agent memory is usually discussed in isolation, leaving the flow of information between storage and computation insufficiently understood. This survey reframes long-context compression as a memory-management problem for allocating inference-time resources. We distinguish compute-active memory, which directly participates in model computation, from compute-inactive memory, which lies outside the computation path but can be stored, retrieved, updated, or forgotten, and we characterize offloaded KV caches, persistent prefix caches, and precomputed KV states as intermediate states on the activation path. On this basis, we formulate writing, offloading, recall, retrieval-and-injection, materialization, eviction, consolidation, and forgetting as state transitions within an information lifecycle, and introduce a budgeted cost model that places these operations in a single cost account. The resulting framework unifies short-term context compression, long-term memory management, and the transitions between them, and shows that the central challenge is not merely reducing information volume but deciding which information should participate in costly inference, when, and in what form. We further summarize evaluation dimensions, benchmark gaps, and open challenges, and argue that future research should move from static, pointwise compression toward dynamic, governable, lifecycle-aware memory management.

Title: Language Models Share a Readout Bottleneck in Working Memory

  • Authors: Dongyu Gong
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL, q-bio.NC
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21176
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.21176
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.21176]] Language Models Share a Readout Bottleneck in Working Memory(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21176)
  • Keywords: transformer, large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) reproduce a human working-memory signature on the N-back task: performance declines as memory load N increases, and errors concentrate on lures, items that repeat the current stimulus at a neighboring but incorrect serial position. We ask whether this shared behavioral signature reflects a shared mechanism. The literature offers two candidate accounts. On a precision account, softmax attention cannot concentrate its weight on the item N positions back, so adjacent items are retrieved instead. On an interference account, attention selects the correct item, but the content of competing items corrupts the decision. We dissociate these accounts in 15 pretrained LLMs from three families (70 million to 14 billion parameters) and in minimal transformers trained from scratch. In all three families, the attention heads that carry the task attend to the target on most missed matches. Linear probes decode near-perfectly whether a matching letter occurred at the target or at a lure position, even on trials where the model false-alarms to the lure, although its own decisions barely distinguish the two. Sharpening attention yields negligible gains, whereas suppressing the letter identity of non-target items improves sensitivity (d') in nearly all model-by-load conditions. In larger models the task is spread over more heads, and removing interference helps more. In minimal transformers, precision limits a single attention layer, but a second layer removes this constraint. The working-memory limit of LLMs therefore lies in reading items out under interference rather than in locating them, consistent with interference accounts of human working memory.

  • Abstract: High-resolution synthesis with Latent Diffusion Models (LDMs) faces a fundamental trade-off between computational cost and generative fidelity. While aggressive compression (f32) significantly improves efficiency, it suffers from severe information loss and ambiguity compared to standard f8 models.   • Abstract: Point-cloud streams are central to autonomous driving and robotics, yet existing segmentation networks process every frame from scratch, although consecutive frames largely overlap. Building on this, we present SpaceCache, a training-free feature cache that reuses these features across frames. Tokens of previously observed regions skip a sequence of transformer blocks and take their cached features, only the remaining tokens are computed, and the full token layout is restored for dense prediction. Title: On the Complexity of Symmetric Boolean Functions in Multiparty Private Simultaneous Messages

  • Authors: Marshall Ball, Peter Crawford-Kahrl, Vladimir Podolskii, Morgan Prior
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21216
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.21216
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.21216]] On the Complexity of Symmetric Boolean Functions in Multiparty Private Simultaneous Messages(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21216)
  • Keywords: secure, privacy
  • Abstract: Multiparty Private Simultaneous Messages (PSM), also known as Decomposable Randomized Encoding (DRE) or information-theoretic garbling, is a model for non-interactive secure computation. Each party encodes its input using shared randomness and sends a message to an evaluator who does not know that randomness. The evaluator must recover the function output without learning anything else about the inputs. Perfect privacy requires identical transcript distributions on inputs with the same output. We also study the weaker notion of support privacy, which requires only that these distributions have the same support. We study the communication complexity of symmetric Boolean functions when each of the $n$ parties holds one input bit. Lower bounds. We prove a superlinear lower bound for support-private PSM protocols computing $OR_n$. Our technique gives $\Omega(n\log n)$ lower bounds for $MAJ_n$ and $EMAJ_n$ (with even $n$), and an $\Omega(n\log q)$ lower bound for $MOD_q$ when $q\leq(1-c)n$ for a fixed constant $c>0$. Upper bounds. We construct perfectly private PSM protocols of total communication $\widetilde{O}(n^{3/2})$ for every symmetric Boolean function, improving on the known quadratic bound. For $MAJ_n$, known branching-program constructions yield an upper bound of $O(n\log^3 n\log\log n)$. For $EMAJ_n$ and $MOD_q$ with prime $q$, the lower bounds are tight up to constant factors in the stated regimes. Under support privacy, we obtain $\widetilde{O}(n)$ communication for every symmetric Boolean function. Earlier lower-bound techniques of Ball et al. (ITCS 2020) yield only trivial linear bounds for symmetric Boolean functions. Concurrent and independent work by Holmgren and Wu proves a $\omega(n)$ lower bound for $OR_n$ using the same combinatorial core. Both works used AI assistance.

Title: Not All Change Is Damage: Update-Matched Unlearning for Large Language Models

  • Authors: Jie Li, Youming Tao, Bowei He, Meng Ding
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21230
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.21230
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.21230]] Not All Change Is Damage: Update-Matched Unlearning for Large Language Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21230)
  • Keywords: privacy, defense, robust, large language model
  • Abstract: Unlearning aims to remove selected knowledge from large language models to mitigate privacy and safety risks while preserving useful capabilities. For models that continue to be fine-tuned after release, this goal is insufficient: they must also resist relearning the deleted knowledge while preserving plasticity, the ability to learn new tasks. Existing defenses train against simulated relearning while trying to preserve useful capabilities, but low relearning alone cannot distinguish selective resistance from a general loss of plasticity. Evaluating preservation after an update requires deciding what the updated model should remain close to. A pre-update reference penalizes useful changes induced by the update simply because they depart from earlier outputs. We therefore formulate the reference-timing hypothesis that, at comparable target suppression, an update-matched reference better preserves plasticity than a pre-update one. To test and address this hypothesis, we propose Update-Matched Unlearning (UMU), which applies the same simulated relearning update to the defended model and to an undefended reference initialized from the same unlearned model, and uses new-task learning to guide model selection. Extensive experiments on TOFU and FaithUn with Llama and Qwen models show that UMU improves both relearning resistance and plasticity across all three base unlearning methods, lowering harmful and benign relearning by 30.4 and 9.9 points on average while raising new-task accuracy by 3.5 points. When the reference does not receive the update, relearning remains similarly low but new-task accuracy falls below the base method, supporting the reference-timing hypothesis and showing that robust unlearning should prevent recovery selectively without suppressing learning itself.

  • Keywords: robust, segmentation
  • Abstract: A few well-annotated datasets for land-cover semantic segmentation have recently been introduced to advance the field of earth observation technologies. To enhance land-cover semantic segmentation with improved generalization ability, we conducted the first investigation into the impact of climate on this task. These images are gathered from four distinct climate zones, namely temperate monsoon, subtropical monsoon, tropical monsoon, and tropical rainforest. This characteristic makes CASID the first climate-aware land-cover semantic segmentation dataset with multiple domains. Additionally, we provide a comprehensive analysis of the samples from the four climate zones, emphasizing differences in global image features, image texture, category distribution, spectral value, and object shape. These analyses offer valuable insights for subsequent research in this field. Moreover, we conduct extensive experiments to evaluate the latest semantic segmentation and unsupervised domain adaptation methods on the CASID dataset. These results serve as a robust baseline for future research endeavors. Our dataset will be made publicly available at this https URL.

Title: System1-Arena: Which Decisions Can Be Left to System 1?

  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21244

  • Keywords: generative, large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) often make discrete decisions, such as flagging phishing or escalating to a human.
